A New Structure for the 2D water wave equation: Energy stability and Global well-posedness
Qingtang Su, Siwei Wang

Abstract
. We study the two-dimensional gravity water waves with a one-dimensional interface with small initial data. Our main contributions include the development of two novel localization lemmas and a Transition-of-Derivatives method, which enable us to reformulate the water wave system into the following simplified structure: ( D t 2 − i A ∂ α ) θ = i t α | D t 2 ζ | 2 D t θ + R (D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha})\theta=i\frac{t}{\alpha}|D_{t}^{2}\zeta|^{2}D_{t}\theta+R where R R behaves well in the energy estimate. As a key consequence, we derive the uniform bound (0.1) sup t ≥ 0 ( ‖ D t ​ ζ ​ ( ⋅ , t ) ‖ H s + 1 / 2 + ‖ ζ α ​ ( ⋅ , t ) − 1 ‖ H s ) ≤ C ​ ϵ , \sup_{t\geq 0}\Big(\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{H^{s+1/2}}+\left\lVert\zeta_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)-1\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\Big)\leq C\epsilon, which enhances existing global uniform energy estimates for 2D water waves by imposing less restrictive constraints on the low-frequency components of the initial data.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

The goal of this paper is to study the structure of the two dimensional water waves and its long time behavior. When surface tension and viscosity are neglected, and the water depth is assumed to be infinite, the motion of irrotational and incompressible two-dimensional water waves is governed by the following equations:

(1.1)

\begin{cases}v_{t}+v\cdot\nabla v=-\nabla P-(0,1)\ \text{in}\ \Omega(t)\\
div\penalty\ v=0,\penalty\ curl\penalty\ v=0\ \text{in}\ \Omega(t)\\
P\Big|_{\Sigma(t)}\equiv 0\\
(1,v)\text{ is tangent to the free surface }(t,\Sigma(t)),\end{cases}

where
v
is the fluid velocity,
P
is the fluid pressure.

From
{\rm div\,}v=0
and
{\rm curl\,}v=0
, it follows that
\bar{v}
is holomorphic in
\Omega(t)
. Consequently,
v
is fully determined by its boundary value on
\Sigma(t)
. Let the interface
\Sigma(t)
be parametrized by
z=z(\alpha,t)
, where
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
serves as the Lagrangian coordinate, chosen such that
z_{t}(\alpha,t)=v(z(\alpha,t),t)
.

Thus, we have
v_{t}+v\cdot\nabla v\Big|_{\Sigma(t)}=z_{tt}
. Since
P(z(\alpha,t),t)\equiv 0
, the gradient of the pressure along the interface can be expressed as

\nabla P\Big|_{\Sigma(t)}=-iaz_{\alpha},

where
a:=-\frac{\partial P}{\partial n}\frac{1}{|z_{\alpha}|}
is a real-valued function.

Therefore, the motion of
\Sigma(t)
can be written as

(1.2)

z_{tt}-iaz_{\alpha}=-i.

Since
\bar{z}_{t}
represents the boundary value of
\bar{v}
,
the water wave equations (
1.1
) can be equivalently expressed as:

(1.3)

\begin{cases}z_{tt}-iaz_{\alpha}=-i\\
\bar{z}_{t}\penalty\ \text{is}\penalty\ \text{holomorphic},\end{cases}

where the holomorphicity of
\bar{z}_{t}
implies the existence of a bounded holomorphic function
\mathcal{V}(\cdot,t)
on
\Omega(t)
, satisfying
\mathcal{V}(x+iy,t)\rightarrow 0
as
y\rightarrow-\infty
, such that
\bar{z}_{t}(\alpha,t)=\mathcal{V}(z(\alpha,t),t)
.

The Taylor sign condition (often referred to as Rayleigh-Taylor sign conditio)
-\frac{\partial P}{\partial\vec{n}}>0
on the pressure is a crucial stability criterion for the water waves problem. When this condition fails, the system is generally unstable; see
[
5
,
8
,
40
,
20
,
38
]
for examples. In the irrotational case, Nalimov
[
31
]
, Yosihara
[
54
]
and Craig
[
16
]
proved local well-posedness for 2d water waves equation for small initial data. In the irrotational case without a bottom, Wu
[
48
,
49
]
demonstrated the validity of the Taylor sign condition, a result crucial to establishing the first local-in-time existence theorems for large data in Sobolev spaces. Since then, a lot of interesting local well-posedness results were obtained, see for example
[
2
,
4
,
12
,
13
,
27
,
29
,
30
,
32
,
35
,
55
]
, and the references therein. See also
[
34
,
47
,
46
,
52
]
for water waves with non-smooth interfaces. For the formation of splash singularities, see for example
[
10
,
9
,
14
,
15
]
.
For cases with non-trivial vorticity or a bottom, the Taylor sign condition may fail and must be assumed for the initial data. Regarding the local-in-time well-posedness with regular vorticity, see
[
26
,
32
,
33
,
12
,
12
,
30
,
55
]
and
[
36
]
for water waves with point vortices.

To study the long time behavior of the water waves, we need a deeper understanding of its structure. It is known since the work of Dyachenko and Zakharov
[
19
]
that for the weakly nonlinear 2d
infinite depth water waves, there are no 3-wave interactions and all of the 4-wave interaction coefficients
vanish on the non-trivial resonant manifold. This means that, in terms of energy, there are no cubic terms in the nonlinearity. Dyachenko and Zakharov’s work is formal. A rigorous justification of Dyachenko and Zakharov’s observation is given by Berti, Feola, and Pusateri in
[
6
]
.

In the irrotational case, in
[
50
]
, Wu found an explicit cubic structure for the 2d water waves:

(D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha})\theta=-2\left[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}\right]\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\zeta+\frac{1}{\pi i}\int\Big(\frac{D_{t}\zeta(\alpha)-D_{t}\zeta(\beta)}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}\Big)^{2}\partial_{\beta}(\zeta-\bar{\zeta})\,d\beta

(Here
\mathcal{H}
is defined as the Hilbert transform associated to
\zeta
. We refer the readers to the relevant part in
[
50
]
or Chapter 2; such symbol will appear frequently in the following discussion). This cubic structure confirms the absence of three-wave interactions, and using this, Wu is able to prove the almost global well-posedness of the 2d gravity water waves with small and localized initial data. See §
1.3.3
for more details.

In
[
53
]
, directly in the physical space, Wu constructed a sequence of energy functionals
E_{j}(t)
which are
explicit in the Riemann mapping variable and involve material derivatives of order
j
of the solutions for the
2d water wave equation, so that

\frac{dE_{j}(t)}{dt}
is quintic or higher order. See also
[
23
,
24
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
56
,
1
]
. We also mention
[
25
,
7
,
21
,
22
]
and
[
36
]
for some investigations in the rotational case.

In
[
42
]
, Totz and Wu rigorously justify the NLS approximation of the 2d water waves. They are able to construct water waves of the form

\zeta(\alpha,t)=\alpha+\epsilon B(X,T)e^{ik\alpha+i\omega t}+O(\epsilon^{2}),

where
\zeta
the modified Lagrangian coordinates of the free surface,
B
solves the 1d cubic NLS,
X=\epsilon(\alpha-\omega^{\prime}t)
, and
T=\epsilon^{2}t
,
k
and
\omega
are constants, satisfying
\omega^{2}=k
. They show that this approximation is valid at least on a time scale of order
O(\epsilon^{-2})
. If we insert this ansatz back into the water wave system, then the water wave system can be reformulated in the following form:

(1.4)

(D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha})\theta=2ik^{3/2}|D_{t}\zeta|^{2}D_{t}\zeta+O(\epsilon^{4}):=G_{1}+O(\epsilon^{4}).

It’s remarkable that the leading order term
G_{1}
in (
1.4
) is simple and explicit. Moreover, if we define the natural energy associated to (
1.4
)

E(t):=\int\frac{1}{A}|D_{t}\zeta|^{2}-i\theta_{\alpha}\bar{\theta},

then the leading order term
G_{1}
disappears in the energy estimates.

Based on this observation, in this paper, we show that such a nice structure is true in a more general setting. In particular, if the initial data is smooth, small, and localized, then we have

(D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha})\theta=i\frac{t}{\alpha}|D_{t}^{2}\zeta|^{2}D_{t}\theta+R

where

(I-\mathcal{H})R=O(\epsilon^{3}t^{-1-\delta})_{L^{2}}.

As a key consequence, we derive the uniform bound

(1.5)

\sup_{t\geq 0}\Big(\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{H^{s+1/2}}+\left\lVert\zeta_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)-1\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\Big)\leq C\epsilon,

1.2.
Main theorem

Denote

\xi_{0}=z(\alpha,0)-\alpha,\quad v_{0}=z_{t}(\alpha,0).

Our main theorem is as follows.

Theorem 1.1

(Main theorem)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\geq 10
. Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
be sufficiently small. For all
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}
, assume

(1.6)

\left\lVert v_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{s+1/2}}+\left\lVert\xi_{0}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1/2}}+\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}\xi_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{s}}+\left\lVert\alpha\partial_{\alpha}v_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{s-1/2}}+\left\lVert\alpha\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\xi_{0}\right\rVert_{H^{s-1}}\leq\epsilon

(1)

We can reformulate the water wave equation in the following form

(1.7)

(D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha})\theta=i\frac{t}{\alpha}|D_{t}^{2}\zeta|^{2}D_{t}\theta+R,

where

(I-\mathcal{H})R=O(\epsilon^{3}t^{-1-\delta})_{L^{2}}.

(2)

The water wave system (
1.3
) is globally wellposed and moreover, there exists a constant
C
depends on
s
only, such that

(1.8)

\sup_{t\geq 0}(\left\lVert z_{t}\right\rVert_{H^{s+1/2}}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1/2}}+\left\lVert z_{\alpha}-1\right\rVert_{H^{s}}+\left\lVert z_{tt}\right\rVert_{H^{s}})\leq C\epsilon,

and for all
t\geq 1
,

(1.9)

\left\lVert z_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)-1\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}+\left\lVert z_{tt}(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}+\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}z_{t}\right\rVert_{W^{s-3,\infty}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-1/2}.

Remark 1.2
.

We emphasize that:

•

This result implies the nonlinear stability of the equilibrium state.

•

Compared to
[
43
]
and
[
1
]
, we assume
z_{t}\in H^{s+1/2}
, whereas
[
43
]
essentially assumes
|\partial_{\alpha}|^{-3/10}z_{t}\in H^{s^{\prime}}
and
[
1
]
assumes
|\partial_{\alpha}|^{-1/4}z_{t}\in H^{s^{\prime}}
. Our assumptions on the initial data are less restrictive for low frequencies.

•

Notably, we prove an almost conservation law: let
\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)
be an appropriately defined
s
-th order energy, with
\mathcal{E}_{s}(0)\sim\epsilon^{2}
. We have

(1.10)

\sup_{t\geq 0}|\mathcal{E}_{s}(t)-\mathcal{E}_{s}(0)|\lesssim\epsilon^{4}.

•

The assumption that
s\geq 10
is certainly not optimal. In fact,
s\geq 4
would suffice. Reducing the value of
s
is not the primary focus of this paper.

1.3.
The difficulty and the main idea

1.3.1.
The main difficulty

The water wave equations are nonlocal, quasilinear, and governed by a free boundary, making their structure inherently complex and difficult to fully understand. In
[
50
]
, Wu uncovered that the water wave system possesses a cubic structure, which she leveraged to prove the almost global well-posedness of the 2D water wave equations. Building on this insight, Ionescu and Pusateri established global well-posedness by combining Wu’s cubic structure with the technique of modified scattering. Around the same time, Alazard and Delort employed the paradifferential calculus to obtain the delicate
L^{2}
to
L^{\infty}
decay estimates and achieve global well-posedness. To elucidate the underlying ideas, we consider a simplified toy model. Let
f\in C^{\infty}
be given. Suppose we are given a PDE system:

(1.11)

\begin{cases}u_{tt}+|\partial_{\alpha}|u=f(|u_{t}|^{2})u_{t}\\
\left\lVert u_{t}(t=0)\right\rVert_{H^{s}}+\left\lVert|\partial_{\alpha}|^{1/2}u(t=0)\right\rVert_{H^{s}}=\epsilon\ll 1.\end{cases}

Suppose
u_{t}
has pointwise decay
\left\lVert u_{t}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\epsilon(1+t)^{-1/2}
. Define the energy

E(t)=\left\lVert u_{t}(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\lVert\Lambda u(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Then one has

\frac{dE(t)}{dt}=2Re\int O(|u_{t}|^{2})|u_{t}|^{2}\leq C\epsilon^{2}(1+t)^{-1}E(t).

From which we obtain

(1.12)

E(t)\leq CE(0)t^{C\epsilon^{2}}.

This toy model illustrates the idea of Wu
[
50
]
, Ionescu and Pusateri
[
28
]
, among others. They reformulated the water wave equations into a structure analogous to (
1.11
), enabling them to establish that the energy of the solution satisfies a bound of the form (
1.12
). However, since their analysis does not fully exploit the intricate structure of the nonlinearity, they did not demonstrate that
\sup_{t\geq 0}E(t)<\infty
.

1.3.2.
Our idea

Our main observation is that the nonlinearity of the water wave equation exhibits an additional structure. Specifically, if
f(|u_{t}|^{2})=i|u_{t}|^{2}
, then
E^{\prime}(t)=0
. So
E(t)=E(0)
. For the water wave equations, we can reformulate them into the following form:

(1.13)

\begin{cases}u_{tt}+|\partial_{\alpha}|u=i\frac{t^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}|u_{t}|^{2}u_{t}+O(\epsilon^{2}t^{-1-\delta})_{L^{\infty}}u_{t}\\
\left\lVert u_{t}(t=0)\right\rVert_{H^{s}}+\left\lVert|\partial_{\alpha}|^{1/2}u(t=0)\right\rVert_{H^{s}}=\epsilon\ll 1.\end{cases}

Let
E(t)
be the energy as defined above. Then,

\frac{dE(t)}{dt}=2Re\int\bar{u}_{t}\Big(i\frac{t^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}|u_{t}|^{2}u_{t}+O(\epsilon^{2}t^{-1-\delta})_{L^{\infty}}u_{t}\Big)=2Re\int\bar{u}_{t}O(t^{-1-\delta})_{L^{\infty}}u_{t}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}t^{-1-\delta}E(t).

From this, we conclude that
\sup_{t\geq 0}E(t)\lesssim E(0)
, thereby proving the nonlinear stability of the equilibrium.

It is noteworthy that the leading term in the nonlinearity is local. This emerges naturally from our ”localization lemma,” which effectively reduces nonlocal terms to local ones.

The key elements in establishing
\sup_{t\geq 0}E(t)\lesssim E(0)
are as follows.

(1)

Cubic Nonlinearity: The nonlinearity of the system consists of cubic and higher-order terms.

(2)

Good Structure: The leading-order term in the nonlinearity cancels out in the energy estimates, simplifying the analysis.

(3)

Decay estimates:
\left\lVert u_{t}(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\epsilon t^{-1/2}
, ensuring sufficient decay over time.

If the water wave equations satisfy these three conditions, a similar approach can be applied to establish their nonlinear stability.

In the following, we detail how to verify these three properties for the water wave equations.

1.3.3.
Change of coordinates

In
[
50
]
, Wu introduced the modified Lagrangian coordinates to demonstrate that the water wave equations possess a cubic structure. We briefly outline her approach below.

Let
\kappa(\cdot,t):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a diffeomorphism. Denote
\zeta:=z\circ\kappa^{-1}
. The diffeomorphism
\kappa
is chosen such that
\bar{\zeta}-\alpha
is holomorphic in the sense described earlier.

By composing the equation (
1.3
) with the diffeomorphism
\kappa^{-1}
, we find that
\zeta
satisfies

(1.14)

\begin{cases}(D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha})\zeta=-i\\
D_{t}\bar{\zeta},\quad\bar{\zeta}-\alpha\penalty\ \text{are}\penalty\ \text{holomorphic},\end{cases}

where the following notations are used:

(1.15)

D_{t}=\partial_{t}+b\partial_{\alpha},\,\,b=(\partial_{t}\kappa)\circ\kappa^{-1},\,\,A:=(a\kappa_{\alpha})\circ\kappa^{-1}.

This coordinate system (which we call
Wu’s coordinate
) was first introduced in
[
50
]
to establish the almost global well-posedness of 2D water waves with small, localized data. It has since been employed in works such as
[
51
,
42
,
41
,
37
,
36
,
28
,
11
]
, to study water wave problems on longer time scales.

Once the existence of such coordinates is established, the water wave system can be directly analyzed in this framework without reverting to the original Lagrangian coordinates. In §
2.4
, by deriving expressions for
b
and
A
, we formulate the water wave system in terms of the
\zeta
variables and avoid explicitly using the change of variables
\kappa
. By Corollary
4.4
,
\left\lVert\kappa_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)-\kappa_{\alpha}(\cdot,0)\right\rVert_{\infty}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}
, so the change of variables can be uniformly controlled over time.

\bullet
Cubic structure: In Wu’s Lagrangian coordinates, Wu
[
50
]
finds the following cubic structure:

(1.16)

(D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha})(I-\mathcal{H})(\zeta-\alpha)=G,

where
D_{t}=\partial_{t}+b\partial_{\alpha}
with
b
and
A-1
quadratic, and

G=-2\left[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}\right]\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\zeta+\frac{1}{\pi i}\int\Big(\frac{D_{t}\zeta(\alpha,t)-D_{t}\zeta(\beta,t)}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}\Big)^{2}(\bar{\zeta}_{\beta}-1)d\beta:=G_{1}+G_{2}.

\bullet
The energy functional

Let
\theta=(I-\mathcal{H})(\zeta-\alpha)
. Define energy

E_{0}^{\theta}(t)=\int\frac{1}{A}|D_{t}\theta|^{2}d\alpha+i\int\theta\partial_{\alpha}\bar{\theta}d\alpha

Then we have

(1.17)

\frac{dE_{0}^{\theta}}{dt}=Re\left\{\int\frac{2D_{t}\bar{\theta}}{A}Gd\alpha\right\}-\int\frac{|D_{t}\theta|^{2}}{A}\left(\frac{a_{t}}{a}\circ\kappa^{-1}\right)d\alpha

We will demonstrate that
\frac{a_{t}}{a}\circ\kappa^{-1}
decays at a rate of
t^{-1-\delta}
(refer to Proposition
4.3
). Thus, it remains to address
G
.

1.3.4.
The good structure of
G

Our key observation is the following decomposition for the nonlinearities (see Proposition
4.4
).

Proposition 1.1
.

We have

(1.18)

\left\lVert(I-\mathcal{H})G_{1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\epsilon^{3}t^{-1-\delta},

and

(1.19)

G_{2}=i\frac{t}{\alpha}|D_{t}^{2}\zeta|^{2}D_{t}\theta+e,\quad with\quad\left\lVert e\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\epsilon^{3}t^{-1-\delta}.

The key idea in proving Proposition
1.1
is a
localization lemma
, which stems from the
profile decomposition
described below.

Denote

I(f,g,h)=\frac{1}{\pi i}\int\frac{(f(\alpha)-f(\beta))(g(\alpha)-g(\beta))}{(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}\bar{h}_{\beta}d\beta.

Where
f,g,h
are anti-holomorphic. For the linear water wave equations
u_{tt}-iu_{\alpha}=0
, the leading order term of
u(\alpha,t)
is
\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}e^{-it^{2}/4\alpha}\phi(\frac{t^{2}}{\alpha^{2}})
. While
u
and its derivatives have decay
t^{-1/2}
in
L^{\infty}
, the profile
U:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\phi(\frac{t^{2}}{\alpha^{2}})
satisfies

\left\lVert\partial_{t}U\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim t^{-1},\quad\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}U\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim t^{-1}.

This motivates us to make the profile decomposition

f(\alpha,t)=e^{-it^{2}/4\alpha}F(\alpha,t),\quad g(\alpha,t)=e^{-it^{2}/4\alpha}G(\alpha,t),\quad h(\alpha,t)=e^{-it^{2}/4\alpha}H(\alpha,t).

Assume a priori that for some small
\delta>0

(1.20)

\left\lVert F_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+\left\lVert G_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+\left\lVert H_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\epsilon t^{-1+\delta}

Then we have

(1.21)

\begin{split}I(f,g,h)(\alpha,t)=&FGH(\alpha,t)\frac{1}{\pi i}\int\frac{(e^{-it^{2}/4\alpha}-e^{-it^{2}/4\beta})^{2}}{(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}e^{it^{2}/4\beta}d\beta+O(t^{-1-\delta})_{L^{2}}\\
=&\frac{it^{2}}{2\alpha^{2}}FGH(\alpha,t)e^{-it^{2}/4\alpha}+O(t^{-1-\delta})_{L^{2}}\\
=&\frac{it^{2}}{2\alpha^{2}}fgh(\alpha,t)+O(t^{-1-\delta})_{L^{2}},\end{split}

where
O(t^{-1-\delta})_{L^{2}}
means the
L^{2}
norm of this term decays like
t^{-1-\delta}
. Refer to Lemma
4.14
and Lemma
4.15
for detailed explanations and proofs. By applying these localization lemmas to the nonlinearities
G=G_{1}+G_{2}
, we derive Proposition
1.1
.

Remark 1.3
.

To rigorously justify (
1.20
), note that

(1.22)

F_{\alpha}=e^{it^{2}/4\alpha}\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha}L_{0}f-\frac{t}{2\alpha^{2}}\Omega_{0}f\Big).

So it suffices to guarantee that
\left\lVert L_{0}f\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+\left\lVert\Omega_{0}f\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\epsilon t^{\delta}
. Therefore, in order to apply the localization lemma to
\partial_{\alpha}^{s}I(f,g,h)
, we need to control
\left\lVert L_{0}f\right\rVert_{H^{s-1}}
,
\left\lVert\Omega_{0}f\right\rVert_{H^{s-1}}
, among others. This is why we need to control
L_{0}D_{t}\zeta
and
L_{0}D_{t}^{2}\zeta
up to
s-1
derivatives.

Applying Proposition
1.1
to (
1.17
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{dE_{0}^{\theta}}{dt}=Re\int\frac{2D_{t}\bar{\theta}}{A}\Big(i\frac{t}{\alpha}|D_{t}^{2}\zeta|^{2}D_{t}\theta\Big)+Re\int\frac{2D_{t}\bar{\theta}}{A}e-\int\frac{|D_{t}\theta|^{2}}{A}\left(\frac{a_{t}}{a}\circ\kappa^{-1}\right)d\alpha

Note that the first integral vanishes. Since
\left\lVert\frac{a_{t}}{a}\circ\kappa^{-1}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{-1-\delta}
(see Proposition
4.3
), we obtain

\frac{dE_{0}^{\theta}}{dt}\lesssim\epsilon^{3}t^{-1-\delta}E_{0}^{\theta},

from which it follows that

(1.23)

\sup_{t\geq 0}|E_{0}^{\theta}(t)-E_{0}^{\theta}(0)|\lesssim\epsilon^{4}.

Therefore, the energy
E_{0}^{\theta}(t)
is almost conserved and, in particular, is uniformly bounded over time. We apply the same method to show that the higher-order energies are also almost conserved. See Corollary
5.2
for more details.

Remark 1.4
.

We compare our structure with the previous works.

•

In
[
3
]
, directly in physical space, and in conjunction with a modified scattering approach, Alazard and Delort derive an equation of the form
\partial_{t}W=O(t^{-1-\delta})
which allows them to obtain sharp decay estimates. This enables them to bound the lower-order energies uniformly in time. However, they are unable to achieve the same for the highest-order energy.

•

In
[
28
]
, Ionescu and Pusateri took the advantages of Wu’s structure in
[
50
]
, the normal form, and the modified scattering to prove the global well-posedness. They use Wu’s cubic structure to derive energy estimates, and the sharp decay estimate is obtained by using the normal form together with the modified scattering. The lower-order energies are bounded uniformly in this article. However, they are unable to achieve the same for the highest-order energy.

•

In
[
18
]
, Deng et al. established a novel quartic energy bound through an innovative proof. By combining this result with decay estimates from
[
28
]
, they successfully derived uniform energy estimates at the highest derivative order. Their analysis assumed
h\in H^{s}
, a condition that particularly constrains low-frequency components.

•

In contrast, we employ a different method to handle the difficulties mentioned above. First, our work uses a formulation in the physical space that is more explicit and simpler to manipulate. Additionally, we develop a novel technique, which we term the ”transition of derivatives” to avoid the loss of derivatives (see §
1.3.6
for a more detailed discussion) and to lower the requirement for the low frequency part of the initial data. Also, such a technique, combining with localization lemma, helps us writing out the further structure of the nonlinearity in the equation, which enables us to establish the uniform-in-time bounds for the highest-order energy and overcoming the limitations of earlier methods.

1.3.5.
The decay estimates

The preceding discussion relies on the a priori assumption that

(1.24)

\left\lVert\zeta_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)-1\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}+\left\lVert D_{t}^{2}\zeta(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}+\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{W^{s-3,\infty}}\lesssim\epsilon t^{-1/2}.

To rigorously establish the decay estimate (
1.24
), we divide the spatial domain into two regions and analyze them separately:

(1)
|\alpha|
is far away from
t
. In this region, the solution exhibits better decay. Specifically, when
|\alpha|
is sufficiently large or small compared to
t
, we can use the fact that the solution decays faster. To be precise, for regions where
|\alpha|\geq t^{1+\mu}
or
|\alpha|\leq t^{1-\mu}
, following Proposition 3.1 in
[
50
]
, we have

(1.25)

\alpha^{2}\partial_{\alpha}(e^{it^{2}/4\alpha}f)=e^{it^{2}/4\alpha}\Big(\alpha L_{0}f-\frac{t}{2}\Omega_{0}f\Big),

and

(1.26)

\frac{t^{2}i}{4}f=-\alpha L_{0}f+\frac{t}{2}\Omega_{0}f+\alpha^{2}\partial_{\alpha}f.

(I)

If
|\alpha|\geq t
, by (
1.25
), we have

(1.27)

|f(\alpha,t)|\leq\frac{2}{|\alpha|^{1/2}}\left\lVert L_{0}f(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+\frac{t}{|\alpha|^{3/2}}\left\lVert\Omega_{0}f(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}},

where
L_{0}=\frac{1}{2}t\partial_{t}+\alpha\partial_{\alpha}
and
\Omega_{0}=\alpha\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{2}ti
.

(II)

For
|\alpha|\leq t
, by (
1.26
),

(1.28)

|f(\alpha,t)|\leq\frac{4|\alpha|}{t^{2}}\left\lVert L_{0}f\right\rVert_{H^{1}}+\frac{4}{t}\left\lVert\Omega_{0}f\right\rVert_{H^{1}}+\frac{8|\alpha|^{3/2}}{t^{2}}\left\lVert L_{0}\partial_{\alpha}f\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+\frac{|\alpha|^{1/2}}{t}\left\lVert\Omega_{0}\partial_{\alpha}f\right\rVert_{L^{2}}.

If we had

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(\left\lVert L_{0}f\right\rVert_{H^{1}}+\left\lVert\Omega_{0}f\right\rVert_{H^{1}})\leq C

for some
C
independent of
T
, then we deduce the decay estimate
|f(\alpha,t)|\lesssim t^{-1/2}
. However, for
f=D_{t}\zeta,D_{t}^{2}\zeta
and
\zeta_{\alpha}-1
, we cannot bound
L_{0}f
and
\Omega_{0}f
uniformly in time. Instead,

\left\lVert L_{0}f\right\rVert_{H^{1}}+\left\lVert\Omega_{0}f\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\epsilon t^{\delta}

for some
0<\delta\ll 1
. Therefore, for fixed
\mu>2\delta
and for
|\alpha|\geq t^{1+\mu}
or
|\alpha|\leq t^{1-\mu}
, by (
1.27
) and (
1.28
), we have

|f(\alpha,t)|\lesssim t^{-1/2-\mu/2+\delta_{0}}.

(2) For regions
t^{1-\mu}\lesssim|\alpha|\lesssim t^{1+\mu}
, the estimates from (
1.27
) and (
1.28
) do not provide the sharp
t^{-1/2}
decay. To handle this, we take advantage of the structure of the solution, particularly focusing on the asymptotic behavior.

Using the localization lemma, we reformulate the equation:

(1.29)

(\partial_{t}^{2}-i\partial_{\alpha})D_{t}\theta=-3i\left(\frac{t}{2\alpha}\right)^{3}|D_{t}\zeta|^{2}D_{t}\zeta+O(t^{-5/4-\delta})_{H^{s-1}}.

For
\nu
fixed. Define a functional

\tilde{E}(t):=\int D_{t}^{2}\theta\bar{u}-D_{t}\theta\partial_{t}\bar{u},\quad u=\chi\left(\frac{\alpha-\nu t}{\sqrt{t}}\right)e^{-it^{2}/4\alpha}.

On one hand, we have

(1.30)

\tilde{E}(t)=\sqrt{t}e^{it^{2}/4\alpha}D_{t}^{2}\theta(\nu t,t)+O(\epsilon t^{-1/40}).

On the other hand, we have

(1.31)

\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{E}(t)=\frac{3i|D_{t}^{2}\zeta(\nu t,t)|^{2}}{\nu}\tilde{E}(t)+O(\epsilon^{3}t^{-1-\delta}).

So we obtain

(1.32)

\left\lVert\tilde{E}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}.

Combining (
1.30
)-(
1.31
)-(
1.32
), we obtain

(1.33)

|D_{t}^{2}\theta(\nu t,t)|\lesssim\epsilon t^{-1/2},

for
t^{-1/5}\lesssim|\nu|\lesssim t^{1/5}
. Therefore, we obtain the desired decay of
D_{t}^{2}\theta
for
\alpha
near
t
. The decay estimates for
\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\theta
can be established using a similar approach.

Remark 1.5
.

Similar functional
\tilde{E}(t)
was used in
[
1
]
. However, because we have reformulated the water wave equations in a more efficient manner, the estimate for
\tilde{E}(t)
is significantly simpler in our approach.

1.3.6.
Control of vector fields and the Transition-of -derivatives method

To close the decay estimates and the localization lemma, it remains to control
L_{0}D_{t}\zeta
,
L_{0}D_{t}^{2}\zeta
and
L_{0}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)
. By Remark
1.3
, we need to control
\left\lVert L_{0}f\right\rVert_{H^{s-1}}
for
f=D_{t}\zeta
and
D_{t}^{2}\zeta
.
The estimates for
\Omega_{0}
can be derived from the formula

(1.34)

\Omega_{0}\partial_{\alpha}f=L_{0}\partial_{t}f-\frac{t}{2}(\partial_{t}^{2}-i\partial_{\alpha})f.

We direct readers to Corollary
6.3
for further details. To estimate
L_{0}D_{t}\theta
, we apply
L_{0}
on both sides of the cubic equation (
1.16
) and obtain

(1.35)

\begin{split}&(D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha})L_{0}\theta=L_{0}G+G\\
&-\Big\{L_{0}D_{t}b-(L_{0}b-\frac{1}{2}b)b_{\alpha}\Big\}\partial_{\alpha}\theta+\Big(L_{0}b-\frac{1}{2}b\Big)\Big(D_{t}\partial_{\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\Big)\theta-i(L_{0}A)\partial_{\alpha}\theta.\end{split}

To estimate
\left\lVert L_{0}D_{t}^{2}\theta\right\rVert_{H^{s-1}}
, we must estimate
\left\lVert L_{0}b\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}^{2}\theta\right\rVert_{H^{s-1}}
. The most challenging aspect of this arises from the term
\left\lVert L_{0}b\partial_{\alpha}^{s}D_{t}^{2}\theta\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
, which presents significant difficulties in the analysis. To illustrate this, we can approximately treat
b=D_{t}\zeta(\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1)
. Thus, we have the expression

L_{0}b\partial_{\alpha}^{s}D_{t}^{2}\theta=(\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1)\partial_{\alpha}^{s}D_{t}\theta L_{0}D_{t}\zeta+D_{t}\zeta\partial_{\alpha}^{s}D_{t}\theta L_{0}(\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1).

The more problematic term is the second one, since we have to estimate
\partial_{\alpha}^{s}D_{t}\theta
in
L^{2}
(without any decay), and we also lack decay estimates for
L_{0}(\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1)
. Furthermore, the decay for

D_{t}\zeta
is only of order
t^{-1/4}
, as shown in Lemma
4.2
. Therefore, it appears that

\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta\partial_{\alpha}^{s}D_{t}^{2}\theta L_{0}(\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert L_{0}(\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert D_{t}^{2}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\lesssim\epsilon t^{-1/4}\left\lVert L_{0}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)\right\rVert_{H^{1}}\left\lVert D_{t}^{2}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s}},

which leads to insufficient decay.

To address this issue, we employ a Transition-of-Derivatives method, which we outline below. Using (
1.25
) and (
1.26
), we derive the following lemma (refer to Lemma
4.3
).

Lemma 1.1
.

We have

(1.36)

f(\alpha,t)=-\frac{2\alpha}{it}\partial_{t}f(\alpha,t)+\frac{2}{it}\Omega_{0}f(\alpha,t),

(1.37)

\partial_{t}f(\alpha,t)=-\frac{it}{2\alpha}f(\alpha,t)+\frac{1}{\alpha}\Omega_{0}f(\alpha,t),

(1.38)

f(\alpha,t)=-\frac{4\alpha}{it^{2}}L_{0}f+\frac{2}{it}\Omega_{0}f+\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{it^{2}}\partial_{\alpha}f,

(1.39)

\partial_{\alpha}f(\alpha,t)=\frac{it^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}f+\frac{1}{\alpha}L_{0}f-\frac{t}{2\alpha^{2}}\Omega_{0}f,

Remark 1.6
.

This lemma allows us to transit the derivatives in the following way:

•

For a single function
f
, the lemma is especially useful in the region where
|\alpha|
is close to
t
, since
\partial_{\alpha}f=\frac{it^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}f
plus a fast decay error term
\frac{1}{\alpha}L_{0}f-\frac{t}{2\alpha^{2}}\Omega_{0}f
, provided that
L_{0}f
and
\Omega_{0}f
grow slowly. A similar argument can be applied to
\partial_{t}f
. This capability is particularly advantageous as it enables us to relax the assumptions on the low-frequency components of
f
. It can also be used to relax the assumptions on high-frequency too. We hope to explore this feature in future works.

•

For products like
f\partial_{\alpha}g
, as long as we can control
L_{0}f,\Omega_{0}f
and
L_{0}g,\Omega_{0}g
, we can transit the derivatives of
g
to
f
, incurring only a negligible cost. See Lemma
4.11
, Lemma
4.12
for further details. Using these lemmas, we obtain the estimates

\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta\partial_{\alpha}^{s}D_{t}^{2}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|\geq t^{3/4})}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}t^{-1};

for
|\alpha|<t^{3/4}
, we apply (
1.38
) to obtain

\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|<t^{3/4})}\leq C\epsilon t^{-1+\delta_{0}}\ln t.

Combining them together yields

\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta\partial_{\alpha}^{s}D_{t}^{2}\theta\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-1+\delta_{0}}\ln t

which is sufficient to close the highest order energy estimates for
L_{0}D_{t}^{2}\theta
. For other orders, there holds a sharper estimate since Lemma
4.12
holds for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
in such case.

1.4.
Outline of the paper

In §2, we present fundamental analytical tools and key formulas that will be used throughout the paper. In §3, we formulate a main proposition and demonstrate how it leads to the proof of the main theorem. In §4, under the bootstrap assumptions, we establish estimates for relevant quantities that are crucial for both the energy and decay estimates. In §5, we provide the energy estimates. In §6, we extend these estimates to include the energy associated with vector fields. In §7, we prove the decay estimates for
\alpha
near
t
. Finally, in the appendix, we compile essential tools from harmonic analysis for reference.

1.5.
Notation and convention

Assume
f
a function on the boundary of
\Omega(t)
. By saying
f
holomorphic, we mean
f
is the boundary value of a holomorphic function in
\Omega(t)
.
The commutator
[A,B]=AB-BA
. By
f=O(A)_{L^{2}}
, we mean
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq CA
for some constant
C>0
. By
f=O(A)
, we mean
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq CA
for some constant
C>0
. Throughout this paper, if not specified, a constant
C
always means a constant that depends on
s
only.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
The Hilbert transform and the characterization of holomorphic functions

Definition 2.1

(Hilbert transform)
.

Assume that
z(\alpha)
satisfies

(2.1)

\beta_{0}|\alpha-\beta|\leq|z(\alpha)-z(\beta)|\leq\beta_{1}|\alpha-\beta|,\quad\quad\forall\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R},

where
0<\beta_{0}<\beta_{1}<\infty
are two absolute constants.
We define the Hilbert transform associated to a curve
z(\alpha)
as

(2.2)

\mathfrak{H}f(\alpha):=\frac{1}{\pi i}p.v.\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{z_{\beta}(\beta)}{z(\alpha)-z(\beta)}f(\beta)d\beta.

We use the notation
\mathcal{H}
to denote the Hilbert transform associated with
\zeta
:

\mathcal{H}f(\alpha,t):=\frac{1}{\pi i}p.v.\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\zeta_{\beta}}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}f(\beta,t)d\beta.

The standard Hilbert transform is the Hilbert transform associated with
z(\alpha)=\alpha
, which is denoted by

(2.3)

\mathbb{H}f(\alpha):=\frac{1}{\pi i}p.v.\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta}f(\beta)d\beta.

It’s well-known (see
[
17
]
, Theorem 6) that if
\zeta(\alpha)
satisfies (
2.1
), then
\mathcal{H}
is bounded on
L^{2}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Assume that
\zeta(\alpha)
satisfies (
2.1
), then

(2.4)

\|\mathcal{H}f\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}},

for some constant that depends on
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{1}
only.

We can use the Hilbert transform to characterize the boundary value of holomorphic functions.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
f
is the boundary value of a holomorphic function in
\Omega(t)
if and only if
(I-\mathcal{H})f=0
.
f
is the boundary value of a holomorphic function in
\Omega(t)^{c}
if and only if
(I+\mathcal{H})f=0
.

2.2.
Water waves in Wu’s coordinates

As discussed in the introduction, we formulate the water waves by the following

(2.5)

\begin{cases}(D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha})\zeta=-i\\
D_{t}\bar{\zeta},\penalty\ \penalty\ \bar{\zeta}(\alpha,t)-\alpha\quad\text{holomorphic}\end{cases}

where

(2.6)

D_{t}:=\partial_{t}+b\partial_{\alpha}

for some real-valued function
b
.

By Lemma
2.2
, due to the holomorphic conditions in (
2.5
), we have

(2.7)

(I-\mathcal{H})D_{t}\bar{\zeta}=0,

and

(2.8)

(I-\mathcal{H})(\bar{\zeta}(\alpha,t)-\alpha)=0.

2.3.
Boundary value of the velocity potential

Note that we have Bernoulli equation (define
\phi
as the velocity potential, i.e.
\nabla\phi=v
.)

(2.9)

\phi_{t}+\frac{1}{2}|v|^{2}+y=P.

Define
\psi=\phi(z(\alpha,t),t)
and
Q=(I-\mathcal{H})(\psi\circ\kappa^{-1})
, then we have

Proposition 2.1
.

(2.10)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=D_{t}\bar{\zeta}+(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)D_{t}\bar{\zeta}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}\right)(\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}),

and

(2.11)

D_{t}Q=-[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{Re\{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}D_{t}\zeta\}}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+\frac{1}{2}(I-\mathcal{H})|D_{t}\zeta|^{2}-\frac{1}{2i}(I-\mathcal{H})(\zeta-\bar{\zeta}).

Proof.

We first prove (
2.11
). Recall that

\phi_{t}+\frac{1}{2}|z_{t}|^{2}+y=P,

and we restrict it to the boundary to obtain

\psi_{t}-\frac{1}{2}|z_{t}|^{2}+y=0,

since the pressure vanishes on the boundary. This implies

D_{t}(\psi\circ\kappa^{-1})-\frac{1}{2}|D_{t}\zeta|^{2}+\eta=0

where
\eta=Im\ \zeta
. Apply both sides by
I-\mathcal{H}
to get

(I-\mathcal{H})D_{t}(\psi\circ\kappa^{-1})=\frac{1}{2}(I-\mathcal{H})|D_{t}\zeta|^{2}-\frac{1}{2i}(I-\mathcal{H})(\zeta-\bar{\zeta});

we further deduce that

\displaystyle(I-\mathcal{H})D_{t}(\psi\circ\kappa^{-1})

\displaystyle=D_{t}Q+[D_{t},\mathcal{H}](\psi\circ\kappa^{-1}),

\displaystyle=D_{t}Q+[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{\partial_{\alpha}(\psi\circ\kappa^{-1})}{\zeta_{\alpha}}.

Since
\nabla\psi(\zeta,t)=v(\zeta,t)=D_{t}\zeta
, we have

(2.12)

\partial_{\alpha}(\psi\circ\kappa^{-1})=\partial_{\alpha}\psi(\zeta(\alpha,t),t)=\nabla\psi(\zeta,t)\cdot\partial_{\alpha}\zeta=Re\left\{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}D_{t}\zeta\right\},

and (
2.11
) is thus proved.

Next we derive (
2.10
). According to (
2.12
) and
\partial_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}f=\zeta_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\frac{\partial_{\alpha}f}{\zeta_{\alpha}}
, there holds

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}(I-\mathcal{H})(\psi\circ\kappa^{-1})=\left(I-\zeta_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}\right)\partial_{\alpha}(\psi\circ\kappa^{-1})=\left(I-\zeta_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}\right)Re\{\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\},

and thus

Re\{\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\}=Q_{\alpha}+\zeta_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}Re\{\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\}=ReQ_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}\right)Re\{\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\}.

Also, since
(I-\mathcal{H})D_{t}\bar{\zeta}=0
, there holds

\left(I-\zeta_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}\right)(\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta})=0,

hence

Q_{\alpha}=-i\left(I-\zeta_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}\right)Im\{\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\}.

Similarly, from such formula we derive that

Im\{\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\}=iQ_{\alpha}+\zeta_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}Im\{\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\}=Re\{iQ_{\alpha}\}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\zeta_{\alpha}\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}\right)Im\{\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\}.

Combining them yields (
2.10
).
∎

2.4.
Formulae for important quantities

To obtain a closed system in (
2.5
), we need formulae for quantities such as
b
,
A
,
D_{t}b
,
\frac{a_{t}}{a}\circ\kappa^{-1}
in terms of the unknown
\zeta
. We record the formulae in the following and refer the readers to section 2 of
[
50
]
.

2.4.1.
Formula for the quantities
b
and
D_{t}b

We have

(2.13)

\begin{split}(I-\mathcal{H})b=&-[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}.\end{split}

And

(2.14)

\begin{split}(I-\mathcal{H})D_{t}b=&[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{\partial_{\alpha}b}{\zeta_{\alpha}}-[D_{t}^{2}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}-[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}}{\zeta_{\alpha}}\\
&+\frac{1}{\pi i}\int\Big(\frac{D_{t}\zeta(\alpha,t)-D_{t}\zeta(\beta,t)}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}\Big)^{2}(\bar{\zeta}_{\beta}(\beta,t)-1)d\beta.\end{split}

So
b
and
D_{t}b
is quadratic.

2.4.2.
The quantity
A

We have

(2.15)

\begin{split}(I-\mathcal{H})A=&1+i[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+i[D_{t}^{2}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}.\end{split}

So
A-1
is quadratic.

2.4.3.
The quantity
\frac{a_{t}}{a}\circ\kappa^{-1}

We have

(2.16)

\begin{split}&(I-\mathcal{H})\frac{a_{t}}{a}\circ\kappa^{-1}A\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}\\
=&2i[D_{t}^{2}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+2i[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}^{2}\bar{\zeta}}{\zeta_{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\Big(\frac{D_{t}\zeta(\alpha,t)-D_{t}\zeta(\beta,t)}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}\Big)^{2}(D_{t}\bar{\zeta})_{\beta}d\beta.\end{split}

So
\frac{a_{t}}{a}\circ\kappa^{-1}
is quadratic.

2.5.
Cubic structure

To obtain the long-time existence of
\zeta
, we need a cubic structure for the system (
2.5
).

Setting
\theta:=(I-\mathcal{H})(\zeta-\bar{\zeta})
. Since
(I-\mathcal{H})(\bar{\zeta}-\alpha)=0
, then one has

(2.17)

\theta=(I-\mathcal{H})(\zeta-\alpha).

In
[
50
]
, Wu derived the following cubic structure.

(2.18)

\begin{split}(D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha})\theta=&-2[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}]\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\zeta+\frac{1}{\pi i}\int\Big(\frac{D_{t}\zeta(\alpha)-D_{t}\zeta(\beta)}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}\Big)^{2}\partial_{\beta}(\zeta-\bar{\zeta})\,d\beta\\
:=&G_{1}+G_{2},\end{split}

where
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are cubic or higher-power nonlinearities.

2.6.
Vector fields and equations for vector fields

We need to use the following vector fields.

Definition 2.2
.

Let

L_{0}=\frac{1}{2}t\partial_{t}+\alpha\partial_{\alpha},\quad\Omega_{0}=\alpha\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{2}ti.

Define

\tilde{\Omega}_{0}:=\bar{\Omega}_{0}=\alpha\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}ti.

Remark 2.1
.

We make the following remarks.

(1)

We use the formula
\Omega_{0}\partial_{\alpha}=L_{0}\partial_{t}-\frac{t}{2}(\partial_{t}^{2}-i\partial_{\alpha})
to derive the estimates for
\Omega_{0}
;

(2)

If
f
is almost anti-holomorphic, then we control
\Omega_{0}f
. If
f
is almost holomorphic, then we control
\tilde{\Omega}_{0}f
.

Let
\mathcal{P}:=D_{t}^{2}-iA\partial_{\alpha}
. We have

(2.19)

\begin{split}[L_{0},\mathcal{P}]=&-\mathcal{P}+\Big\{L_{0}D_{t}b-(L_{0}b-\frac{1}{2}b)b_{\alpha}\Big\}\partial_{\alpha}\\
&+\Big(L_{0}b-\frac{1}{2}b\Big)\Big(D_{t}\partial_{\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\Big)-i(L_{0}A)\partial_{\alpha}.\end{split}

2.7.
Energy functional

Define

(2.20)

E_{k}^{\theta}:=\int\frac{1}{A}|D_{t}\theta_{k}|^{2}+i\theta_{k}\partial_{\alpha}\bar{\theta}_{k}d\alpha.

where
\theta_{k}=\partial_{\alpha}^{k}\theta
. By Wu’s basic energy lemma (lemma 4.1,
[
50
]
), we have

(2.21)

\frac{d}{dt}E_{k}^{\theta}=Re\int\frac{2}{A}D_{t}\bar{\theta}_{k}G_{k}^{\theta}-\int\frac{1}{A}\frac{a_{t}}{a}\circ\kappa^{-1}|D_{t}\theta_{k}|^{2}

Define

(2.22)

E_{k}^{\sigma}:=\int\frac{1}{A}|D_{t}\sigma_{k}|^{2}+i\sigma_{k}\partial_{\alpha}\bar{\sigma}_{k}d\alpha.

where
\sigma_{k}=\partial_{\alpha}^{k}\sigma=\partial_{\alpha}^{k}D_{t}\theta
.
Then we have

(2.23)

\frac{d}{dt}E_{k}^{\sigma}=Re\int\frac{2}{A}D_{t}\bar{\sigma}_{k}G_{k}^{\sigma}-\int\frac{1}{A}\frac{a_{t}}{a}\circ\kappa^{-1}|D_{t}\sigma_{k}|^{2}

Define

(2.24)

\mathcal{E}_{s}:=\sum_{k=0}^{s}(E_{k}^{\theta}+E_{k}^{\sigma}).

Define

(2.25)

E_{L_{0}\theta}^{k}=\int\frac{1}{A}|D_{t}L_{0}\theta_{k}|^{2}+iL_{0}\theta_{k}\partial_{\alpha}L_{0}\bar{\theta}_{k}d\alpha

Define

(2.26)

E_{L_{0}\sigma}^{k}=\int\frac{1}{A}|D_{t}L_{0}\sigma_{k}|^{2}+iL_{0}\sigma_{k}\partial_{\alpha}L_{0}\bar{\sigma}_{k}d\alpha

Define

(2.27)

\mathcal{E}_{s}^{L_{0}}:=\sum_{k=0}^{s-1}(E_{L_{0}\theta}^{k}+E_{L_{0}\sigma}^{k}).

3.
Main proposition and the proof of the Main Theorem

3.1.
Bootstrap assumptions

We make the following bootstrap assumptions. Given
T\gg 1
,
0<\delta_{0}\ll 1
sufficiently small. On
[0,T]
, assume

(1)

(3.1)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big(\left\lVert\Lambda(\zeta-\alpha)\right\rVert_{H^{s}}+\left\lVert D_{t}^{2}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s}}+\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s+1/2}}\Big)\leq 100\epsilon.

(2)

For all
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.2)

\Big(\left\lVert L_{0}D_{t}^{2}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s-2}}+\left\lVert L_{0}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s-3/2}}\Big)\leq 100\epsilon(1+t)^{\delta_{0}},

(3.3)

\Big(\left\lVert L_{0}D_{t}^{2}\zeta\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-1}}+\left\lVert L_{0}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-1}}\Big)\leq 100\epsilon(1+t)^{2\delta_{0}},

and

(3.4)

\left\lVert\Omega_{0}\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{H^{s-2}}+\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{H^{s-3}}\leq 100\epsilon(1+t)^{\delta_{0}},

(3.5)

\left\lVert\Omega_{0}\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-1}}+\left\lVert\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-1}}\leq 100\epsilon(1+t)^{2\delta_{0}}.

(3)

For all
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.6)

\left\lVert(\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\zeta,\zeta_{\alpha}-1,D_{t}^{2}\zeta)\right\rVert_{W^{s-3,\infty}\times W^{s-2,\infty}\times W^{s-2,\infty}}\leq 100\epsilon t^{-1/2}.

3.2.
Main proposition

Proposition 3.1
.

Under the bootstrap assumptions (
3.1
)-(
3.2
)-(
3.6
), we have the improved bounds

(1)

(3.7)

\sup_{t\in[0,T+1]}\Big(\left\lVert\Lambda(\zeta-\alpha)\right\rVert_{H^{s}}+\left\lVert D_{t}^{2}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s}}+\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s+1/2}}\Big)\leq 10\epsilon.

(2)

For all
t\in[0,T+1]
,

(3.8)

\Big(\left\lVert L_{0}D_{t}^{2}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s-2}}+\left\lVert L_{0}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s-2}}\Big)\leq 10\epsilon(1+t)^{\delta_{0}},

(3.9)

\Big(\left\lVert L_{0}D_{t}^{2}\zeta\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-1}}+\left\lVert L_{0}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-1}}\Big)\leq 10\epsilon(1+t)^{2\delta_{0}},

and

(3.10)

\left\lVert\Omega_{0}\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{H^{s-2}}+\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{H^{s-3}}\leq 10\epsilon(1+t)^{\delta_{0}},

(3.11)

\left\lVert\Omega_{0}\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-1}}+\left\lVert\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{s-1}}\leq 10\epsilon(1+t)^{2\delta_{0}}.

(3)

For all
t\in[0,T+1]
,

(3.12)

\left\lVert(\partial_{\alpha}D_{t}\zeta,\zeta_{\alpha}-1,D_{t}^{2}\zeta)\right\rVert_{W^{s-3,\infty}\times W^{s-2,\infty}\times W^{s-2,\infty}}\leq 10\epsilon t^{-1/2}

Proof.

The proposition follows from Corollary
5.1
, Corollary
6.2
, Corollary
6.3
, Corollary
7.3
, Lemma
4.10
, and the local wellposedness of the water wave system.
∎

3.3.
Proof of the main theorem

Given the main proposition, we prove the main theorem.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Denote

(3.13)

\mathcal{T}:=\{T\in[0,\infty):\forall t\in[0,T],\eqref{bootstrap-1}-\eqref{bootstrap-2}-\eqref{bootstrap-2'}-\eqref{bootstrap-3}\penalty\ hold\}.

Since
0\in\mathcal{T}
, so
\mathcal{T}\neq\emptyset
. Clearly,
\mathcal{T}
is a closed set. Let
T\in\mathcal{T}
, by Proposition
3.1
,
\mathcal{T}
is also an open set in
[0,\infty)
. So
\mathcal{T}=[0,\infty)
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
∎

4.
Estimates for relevant quantities

In this section, if not specified, we will always assume the bootstrap assumptions (
3.1
)-(
3.2
)-(
3.3
)-(
3.6
).

First, we show that
\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)
behaves like
\alpha-\beta
in the following sense.

Lemma 4.1
.

We have

(4.1)

\sup_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\frac{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}{\alpha-\beta}-1\Big|\leq C\epsilon(1+t)^{-1/2}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}{\alpha-\beta}-1\Big|=

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\alpha-(\zeta(\beta,t)-\beta)}{\alpha-\beta}\Big|\leq\left\lVert\zeta_{\alpha}-1\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq C\epsilon t^{-1/2}.

∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume the bootstrap assumptions. There holds

(4.2)

\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{4}},

(4.3)

\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{4}}\ln t.

and

(4.4)

\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}D_{t}^{2}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{6}}\ln t.

Proof.

Define
f_{L}:=P_{\leq t^{-1/2}}f
and
f_{H}:=f-f_{L}
. On one hand, according to Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
B.1
),

\left\lVert(D_{t}\zeta)_{L}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq Ct^{-\frac{1}{4}}\left\lVert(D_{t}\zeta)_{L}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{4}};

on the other hand, we have

\left\lVert(D_{t}\zeta)_{H}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq Ct^{\frac{1}{4}}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}(D_{t}\zeta)_{H}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

This proves (
4.2
). By Bernstein’s inequality, and the bootstrap assumptions,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq

\displaystyle\ \left\lVert P_{\leq t^{1/4}}\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\left\lVert P_{\geq t^{1/4}}\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ t^{1/4}\left\lVert P_{\leq t^{1/4}}\partial_{\alpha}^{s-2}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{j\geq 0:2^{j}\leq t^{2}}\left\lVert P_{2^{j}t^{1/4}}\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\left\lVert P_{\geq t^{9/4}}\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ Ct^{1/4-1/2}\epsilon+\sum_{j\geq 0:2^{j}\leq t^{2}}2^{-j}t^{-1/4}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1/2}}+C\epsilon t^{-1}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\epsilon t^{-1/4}\ln t.

This completes the proof of (
4.3
). The proof for (
4.3
) is similar to that of (
4.4
). So the proof of the lemma is completed.
∎

Also, we record some formla that we will use frequently here.

Lemma 4.3
.

We have

(4.5)

f(\alpha,t)=-\frac{2\alpha}{it}\partial_{t}f(\alpha,t)+\frac{2}{it}\Omega_{0}f(\alpha,t),

(4.6)

\partial_{t}f(\alpha,t)=-\frac{it}{2\alpha}f(\alpha,t)+\frac{1}{\alpha}\Omega_{0}f(\alpha,t),

(4.7)

f(\alpha,t)=-\frac{4\alpha}{it^{2}}L_{0}f+\frac{2}{it}\Omega_{0}f+\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{it^{2}}\partial_{\alpha}f,

(4.8)

\partial_{\alpha}f(\alpha,t)=\frac{it^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}f+\frac{1}{\alpha}L_{0}f-\frac{t}{2\alpha^{2}}\Omega_{0}f,

Proof.

Let
F(\alpha,t):=e^{it^{2}/4\alpha}f(\alpha,t)
, then

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F=\partial_{t}(e^{it^{2}/4\alpha}f)=

\displaystyle\frac{it}{2\alpha}e^{it^{2}/4\alpha}f+e^{it^{2}/4\alpha}f_{t}=\frac{1}{\alpha}e^{it^{2}/4\alpha}(\alpha\partial_{t}+\frac{it}{2})f=\frac{1}{\alpha}e^{it^{2}/4\alpha}\Omega_{0}f.

Therefore,

\displaystyle f_{t}=\partial_{t}(e^{-it^{2}/4\alpha}F)=-\frac{it}{2\alpha}f+e^{-it^{2}/4\alpha}F_{t}=-\frac{it}{2\alpha}f+\frac{1}{\alpha}\Omega_{0}f,

which proves (
4.6
). (
4.5
) is equivalent to (
4.6
). (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) follow in a similar way.
∎

Lemma
4.3
allows us to increase or decrease the derivatives of
f
provided that we can control
\Omega_{0}f
and
L_{0}f
. We can use the bootstrap assumptions (
3.2
)-(
3.6
) to estimate
\left\lVert L_{0}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{H^{s-1/2}}
,
\left\lVert L_{0}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)\right\rVert_{H^{s-1}}
,
\left\lVert\Omega_{0}\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{H^{s-1}}
, etc. We cannot directly control
\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}
in
L^{2}
. Instead, we have the following.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any fixed
0<k\leq 2
, we have

(4.9)

\left\lVert\Omega_{0}P_{\geq t^{-k}}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon t^{\delta_{0}}\ln t.

(4.10)

\left\lVert L_{0}P_{\geq t^{-k}}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon t^{\delta_{0}}.

Proof.

We have

\Omega_{0}P_{\geq t^{-k}}Q_{\alpha}=P_{\geq t^{-k}}\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}+[\Omega_{0},P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{\alpha}=P_{\geq t^{-k}}\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}+[\alpha\partial_{t},P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{\alpha}

By the bootstrap assumptions and the Bernstein inequality (Lemma
B.1
), we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{\geq t^{-k}}\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq

\displaystyle\ \sum_{t^{-k}\leq 2^{j}\leq t^{k}}\left\lVert P_{2^{j}}\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{2^{j}>t^{k}}\left\lVert P_{2^{j}}\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \sum_{t^{-k}\leq 2^{j}\leq t^{k}}\left\lVert\Lambda\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+\sum_{2^{j}>t^{k}}2^{-j/2}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\ln t\left\lVert\Lambda\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+t^{-\frac{k}{2}}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\epsilon t^{\delta_{0}}\ln t.

For
[\alpha\partial_{t},P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{\alpha}
, note that

\displaystyle[\alpha\partial_{t},P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{\alpha}=[\alpha,P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{t\alpha}+\alpha[\partial_{t},P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{\alpha};

We have

\displaystyle[\alpha,P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{t\alpha}=\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}[\alpha,P_{2^{j}}]Q_{t\alpha}=\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}i2^{-j}\tilde{P}_{2^{j}}Q_{t\alpha}

where
\tilde{P}
is the Littlewood-Paley operator associated to
\phi^{\prime}
and
\phi
is the notation which is used in Appendix
B
.
By the Bernstein inequality, for
t^{-k}\leq 2^{j}\leq t^{k}
,

(4.11)

\left\lVert[\alpha,P_{2^{j}}]Q_{t\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq 2^{-j}\left\lVert\tilde{P}_{2^{j}}Q_{t\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\left\lVert\Lambda\tilde{P}_{2^{j}}Q_{t}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\left\lVert\Lambda Q_{t}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon.

For
2^{j}\geq t^{k}
, by the Bernstein inequality again, we have

(4.12)

\left\lVert[\alpha,P_{2^{j}}]Q_{t\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq 2^{-\frac{j}{2}}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}Q_{t\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon 2^{-\frac{j}{2}}.

So,

(4.13)

\left\lVert[\alpha,P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{t\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{t^{-k}\leq 2^{j}\leq t^{k}}\left\lVert[\alpha,P_{2^{j}}]Q_{t\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{2^{j}>t^{k}}\left\lVert[\alpha,P_{2^{j}}]Q_{t\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon\ln t.

For
\alpha[\partial_{t},P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{[\partial_{t},P_{\geq t^{-k}}]f}(\xi)=\left(\partial_{t}((1-\phi(t^{k}\xi))\hat{f}(\xi))-(1-\phi(t^{k}\xi))\partial_{t}\hat{f}(\xi)\right)=-kt^{k-1}\xi\phi^{\prime}(t^{k}\xi)\hat{f}(\xi).

Define
\psi(\xi)=\xi\phi^{\prime}(\xi)
(note that
\psi
is supported on an annulus rather than a disk) and let
P^{*}
be the corresponding Littlewood-Paley operator, then

\alpha[\partial_{t},P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{\alpha}=-\alpha kt^{-1}P^{*}_{\geq t^{-k}}Q_{\alpha}=-\alpha kt^{-1}\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}P^{*}_{2^{j}}Q_{\alpha}.

Set
\chi=F^{-1}[\psi]
, then

\displaystyle\alpha kt^{-1}P^{*}_{2^{j}}Q_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\alpha kt^{-1}2^{j}\int\chi(2^{j}(\alpha-\beta))Q_{\beta}d\beta

\displaystyle=kt^{-1}2^{j}\int(\alpha-\beta)\chi(2^{j}(\alpha-\beta))Q_{\beta}d\beta+kt^{-1}2^{j}\int\chi(2^{j}(\alpha-\beta))\beta Q_{\beta}d\beta

\displaystyle\overset{\text{def}}{=}I_{1}+I_{2}.

Let
\lambda(x)=x\chi(x)
and we derive

I_{1}=kt^{-1}\int\lambda(2^{j}(\alpha-\beta))Q_{\beta}d\beta=kt^{-1}2^{-j}P^{**}_{2^{j}}Q_{\alpha}

where
\widehat{P^{**}_{N}f}=F[\lambda](\frac{\xi}{N})\hat{f}(\xi)
. Now we have

|I_{1}|\leq kt^{-1}2^{-j}\left\lVert P^{**}_{2^{j}}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq kt^{-1}2^{-\frac{j}{2}}\left\lVert Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-1}2^{-\frac{j}{2}}

which implies

\left|\alpha kt^{-1}\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}P_{2^{j}}^{*}Q_{\alpha}\right|\leq C\epsilon t^{-1}\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}2^{-\frac{j}{2}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-1+\frac{k}{2}}\leq C\epsilon

since
k\leq 2
.

In addition, note that

I_{2}=kt^{-1}P^{*}_{2^{j}}(\alpha Q_{\alpha})=-kt^{-1}P^{*}_{2^{j}}L_{0}Q+\frac{k}{2}P^{*}_{2^{j}}Q_{t}:=I_{21}+I_{22}

and we have, according to Bernstein’s inequality,

|I_{21}|\leq Ct^{-1}\left\lVert P_{2^{j}}^{*}L_{0}Q\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq Ct^{-1}2^{-\frac{j}{2}}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}L_{0}Q\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-1+\delta_{0}}2^{-\frac{j}{2}}

which implies

\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}|I_{21}|\leq C\epsilon t^{-1+\delta_{0}+\frac{k}{2}}\leq C\epsilon t^{\delta_{0}}.

Finally, for
I_{22}
, we have

|I_{22}|\leq C\left\lVert P^{*}_{2^{j}}Q_{t}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\left\lVert\Lambda Q_{t}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon

and

|I_{22}|\leq C2^{-j}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}Q_{t}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon 2^{-j}.

Combining them yields

\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}|I_{22}|\leq\sum_{t^{-k}\leq 2^{j}\leq t^{k}}|I_{22}|+\sum_{2^{j}>t^{k}}|I_{22}|\leq C\epsilon\ln t+C\epsilon t^{-k}\leq C\epsilon\ln t.

In summary,

\left\lVert\alpha[\partial_{t},P_{\geq t^{-k}}]Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}=\left\lVert\alpha kt^{-1}\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}P^{*}_{2^{j}}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert\leq C\epsilon t^{\delta_{0}}\ln t

which completes the proof for (
4.9
). For (
4.10
), direct calculation gives

[L_{0},P_{j}]=P^{*}_{2^{j}},

hence

\displaystyle\left\lVert L_{0}P_{\geq t^{-k}}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\ C\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}\left\lVert L_{0}P_{2^{j}}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\left(\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}\left\lVert P_{2^{j}}L_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{2^{j}\geq t^{-k}}\left\lVert P^{*}_{2^{j}}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C(\left\lVert L_{0}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\lVert Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\epsilon^{2}t^{2\delta_{0}},

which implies (
4.10
).
∎

Since quantities such as
b
and
A-1
are represented in terms of
(I-\mathcal{H})b
and
(I-\mathcal{H})(A-1)
, we need to control the former by the latter.

First, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 4.5
.

Assume the bootstrap assumptions. We have

(4.14)

\left\lVert(\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{H})f\right\rVert_{H^{s}}+\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}-\mathbb{H}\right)f\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-1/2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{s}},

For any
2\leq q<\infty
, there holds

(4.15)

\left\lVert(\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{H})f\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}+\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}-\mathbb{H}\right)f\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}\leq C(q-1)(\epsilon t^{-1/2})^{1-2/q}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}.

Proof.

We provide details for the estimates for
(\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{H})f
only. The counterparts for
(\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}-\mathbb{H})f
are similar. Indeed,

(\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{H})f=\frac{1}{\pi i}\int\left(\frac{\zeta_{\beta}}{\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)}-\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta}\right)f(\beta)d\beta

and it’s clear that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{|\alpha-\beta|\geq 1}\left(\frac{\zeta_{\beta}}{\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)}-\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta}\right)f(\beta)d\beta\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\int_{|\alpha-\beta|\geq 1}\left|\frac{\zeta_{\beta}-1}{\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)}+\frac{\alpha-\zeta(\alpha)-(\beta-\zeta(\beta))}{(\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta))(\alpha-\beta)}d\beta\right|,

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert\zeta_{\alpha}-1\right\rVert_{L^{q}}\left\lVert\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta}1_{|\alpha-\beta|\geq 1}\right\rVert_{L^{q^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\leq C(q-1)(\epsilon t^{-1/2})^{1-2/q}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}.

On the other hand, we apply Taylor’s expansion to get

\displaystyle\left|\int_{|\alpha-\beta|<1}\left(\frac{\zeta_{\beta}}{\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)}-\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta}\right)f(\beta)d\beta\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \left|\int_{|\alpha-\beta|<1}\left(\frac{\zeta_{\beta}(\alpha-\beta)-(\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta))}{(\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta))(\alpha-\beta)}\right)f(\beta)d\beta\right|,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \left|\int_{|\alpha-\beta|<1}\left(\frac{\zeta_{\beta}(\alpha-\beta)-\zeta_{\alpha}(\alpha-\beta)-\zeta^{\prime\prime}(\xi)(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}{(\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta))(\alpha-\beta)}\right)f(\beta)d\beta\right|,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \left|\int_{|\alpha-\beta|<1}\left(\frac{(\zeta^{\prime\prime}(\eta)-\zeta^{\prime\prime}(\xi))(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}{(\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta))(\alpha-\beta)}\right)f(\beta)d\beta\right|,

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\left\lVert\zeta_{\alpha\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}},

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}.

For higher derivatives, integration by parts, we have

(\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{H})f(\alpha,t)=\frac{1}{\pi i}\int\left(\ln\frac{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}{\alpha-\beta}\right)f_{\beta}(\beta,t)d\beta.

Writing

\partial_{\alpha}^{s-2}=((\partial_{\alpha}+\partial_{\beta})-\partial_{\beta})^{s-2}=(-\partial_{\beta})^{s-2}+\sum_{1\leq k\leq s-2}C_{s-2,k}(\partial_{\alpha}+\partial_{\beta})^{s-2},

we have

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}^{s-2}(\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{H})f(\alpha,t)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi i}\int\Big\{(-\partial_{\beta})^{s-2}\Big(\ln\frac{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}{\alpha-\beta}\Big)\Big\}f_{\beta}(\beta,t)d\beta

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\pi i}\sum_{1\leq k\leq s-2}\int\left\{(\partial_{\alpha}+\partial_{\beta})^{k}(-\partial_{\beta})^{s-2-k}\ln\frac{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}{\alpha-\beta}\right\}f_{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi i}\int\left\{(-\partial_{\beta})\left(\ln\frac{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}{\alpha-\beta}\right)\right\}\partial_{\beta}^{s-2}f(\beta,t)d\beta

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\pi i}\sum_{1\leq k\leq s-2}\int\left\{(\partial_{\alpha}+\partial_{\beta})^{k}\ln\frac{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}{\alpha-\beta}\right\}(-\partial_{\beta})^{s-1-k}f,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle I+II.

Note that
I=(\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{H})\partial_{\alpha}^{s-2}f
. By the argument above, we have

(4.16)

|I|\leq C(q-1)(\epsilon t^{-1/2})^{1-2/q}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s-2}f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}.

Note that
(\partial_{\alpha}+\partial_{\beta})\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta}=0
. A similar argument as above gives

(4.17)

|II|\leq C(q-1)(\epsilon t^{-1/2})^{1-2/q}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Also, we have the following
L^{\infty}
estimates of the Hilbert transform.

Lemma 4.6
.

We have

(4.18)

\left\lVert\mathcal{H}f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq Ct^{-2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{1}}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\ln t.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}f(\alpha,t)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi i}p.v.\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\zeta_{\beta}}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}f(\beta,t)d\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi i}p.v.\int_{|\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)|\leq t^{-4}}\frac{\zeta_{\beta}}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}f(\beta,t)d\beta

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\pi i}p.v.\int_{t^{-4}|\leq\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)|\leq t^{4}}\frac{\zeta_{\beta}}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}f(\beta,t)d\beta

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\pi i}p.v.\int_{|\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)|\geq t^{4}}\frac{\zeta_{\beta}}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}f(\beta,t)d\beta

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle I+II+III.

By the definition of principal values, the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, following by the Hardy’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle I=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{|\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)|\leq t^{-4}}\frac{\zeta_{\beta}}{\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)}\Big(f(\beta,t)-f(\alpha,t)\Big)d\beta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{\pi}\Big(\int\frac{|f(\alpha,t)-f(\beta,t)|^{2}}{(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}d\beta\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{|\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)|\leq t^{-4}}d\beta\Big)^{1/2}\left\lVert\zeta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ Ct^{-2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1}}.

For
II
, using Lemma
4.1
, we have

\displaystyle|II|\leq\int_{t^{-4}\leq|\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)|\leq t^{4}}\frac{1}{|\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)|}d\beta\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\ln t.

For
III
, by the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle|III|\leq C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\int_{|\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)|\geq t^{4}}\frac{1}{|\zeta(\alpha,t)-\zeta(\beta,t)|}d\beta\leq Ct^{-2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}}.

This completes the proof of the Lemma.

∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
f
be a real-valued function. Suppose that
(I-\mathcal{H})f=g
or
(I-\mathcal{H})f\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}=g
, then for
0\leq k\leq s
,

(4.19)

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{k}}\leq C\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{H^{k}};

furthermore, for
0\leq l\leq s-2
,

(4.20)

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{W^{{l},\infty}}\leq C\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{W^{{l},\infty}}.

Proof.

We shall only prove (
4.20
) for
s=0
as others are similar to it. Note that

f=Re\left\{g+(\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{H})f\right\}

and it suffices to control
\left\lVert(\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{H})f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}
. By Lemma
4.5
with
q=2
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\mathcal{H}-\mathbb{H})f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}.

So we conclude that

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+C\epsilon\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

which implies (
4.20
) (for
s=0
and sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
).
∎

Proposition 4.1
.

Assume the bootstrap assumptions. We have

(4.21)

\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta-Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-1/2},\quad\left\lVert 2iD_{t}^{2}\zeta+\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-1/2},

and

(4.22)

\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta-Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{2}{3}}\ln t,\quad\left\lVert 2iD_{t}^{2}\zeta+\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{W^{s-2,\infty}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{2}{3}}\ln t,

Proof.

We first prove the estimate for
\theta
. Note that

\partial_{\alpha}\theta=2\partial_{\alpha}(\zeta-\alpha)+\partial_{\alpha}(\mathcal{H}+\bar{\mathcal{H}})(\zeta-\alpha)=-2iD_{t}^{2}\zeta+(A-1)\zeta_{\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}(\mathcal{H}+\bar{\mathcal{H}})(\zeta-\alpha)

and it’s easy to verify
\left\lVert A-1\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and
\left\lVert A-1\right\rVert_{W^{s-1,\infty}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{3}{4}}\ln t
using Lemma
A.1
. For
\partial_{\alpha}(\mathcal{H}+\bar{\mathcal{H}})(\zeta-\alpha)
, write

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}(\bar{\mathcal{H}}+\mathcal{H})(\zeta-\alpha)

\displaystyle=(\bar{\mathcal{H}}+\mathcal{H})(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)+[\partial_{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{H}}+\mathcal{H}](\zeta-\alpha),

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\int\frac{Im\left\{\zeta_{\beta}(\bar{\zeta}(\alpha)-\bar{\zeta}(\beta))\right\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}(\zeta_{\beta}-1)d\beta+[\zeta_{\alpha},\mathcal{H}]\frac{\zeta_{\alpha}-1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+[\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{H}}]\frac{\zeta_{\alpha}-1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}},

and, by Lemma
A.1
, we have

\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}(\bar{\mathcal{H}}+\mathcal{H})(\zeta-\alpha)\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-1/2}.

Also, since
\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{6}}\ln t
, we derive that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{2}{\pi i}\int\frac{Im\{\zeta_{\beta}(\partial_{\alpha}^{s-2}\bar{\zeta}-\partial_{\beta}^{s-2}\bar{\zeta})\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}(\zeta_{\beta}-1)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\lVert\zeta_{\alpha}-1\right\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}+C\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert\zeta_{\alpha}-1\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\ln t

\displaystyle+Ct^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\left\lVert\zeta_{\alpha}-1\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{2}{3}}\ln t.

This gives the conclusion. For the estimate of
Q_{\alpha}-D_{t}\zeta
, we write (
2.10
) as

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{\alpha}-D_{t}\bar{\zeta}

\displaystyle=(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)D_{t}\bar{\zeta}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}\right)(\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta})-\frac{1}{2}(\zeta_{\alpha}-\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha})\mathcal{H}D_{t}\bar{\zeta},

\displaystyle=(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)D_{t}\bar{\zeta}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}\right)(\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta})-iIm\{\zeta_{\alpha}-1\}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}

and we first deal with the
L^{2}
and
L^{\infty}
cases. It’s easy to verify that

\displaystyle\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta-Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}+C\left\lVert\left(\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}+\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}\right)(\zeta_{\alpha}D_{t}\bar{\zeta})\right\rVert_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}+C\left\lVert\int\frac{Im\{\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}

and

\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta-Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{3}{4}}\ln t

by applying the estimate in Lemma
A.1
.

We treat the
s
order derivatives as follows: for
\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)D_{t}\bar{\zeta}
, it suffices to consider the case where all derivatives apply to
\zeta_{\alpha}-1
. Actually, there holds

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{s}C_{j,s}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{j}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)\partial_{\alpha}^{s-j}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{j=0}^{s-1}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{j}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s-j}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)\bar{Q}_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}+\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\left\lVert\bar{Q}_{\alpha}-D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)P_{\geq t^{-2}}\bar{Q}_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}.

Here we have used Bernstein’s inequality to prove
\left\lVert P_{\leq t^{-2}}\bar{Q}_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-1}
in the last step. For the second term, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)P_{\geq t^{-2}}\bar{Q}_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|\alpha|\leq t)}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon\left\lVert P_{\geq t^{-2}}\bar{Q}_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|\leq t)}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon\left\lVert\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{it^{2}}\partial_{\alpha}P_{\geq t^{-2}}\bar{Q}_{\alpha}-\frac{4\alpha}{it^{2}}L_{0}P_{\geq t^{-2}}\bar{Q}_{\alpha}+\frac{2}{it}\Omega_{0}P_{\geq t^{-2}}\bar{Q}_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|\leq t)}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Here we have used (
4.9
) and (
4.10
). On the other hand, note that according to the
H^{s}
estimate for
\theta_{\alpha}+2iD_{t}^{2}\zeta
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)P_{\geq t^{-2}}\bar{Q}_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|\alpha|>t)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\theta_{\alpha}P_{\geq t^{-2}}\bar{Q}_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|\alpha|>t)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert 2\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)-\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\left\lVert P_{\geq t^{-2}}\bar{Q}_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|>t)}+C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{3}{4}}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Here we have used the formula

\partial_{\alpha}f=\frac{it^{2}}{4\alpha^{2}}f+\frac{1}{\alpha}L_{0}f-\frac{t}{2\alpha^{2}}\Omega_{0}f

twice to lower the derivatives on
\theta
and the estimate
\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s-2}\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. In summary, we have

\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Similarly,
\left\lVert Im\{\zeta_{\alpha}-1\}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and we are left with

\left\lVert\int\frac{Im\{\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{H^{s}}.

With the experience above, we know that it suffices to consider

\left\lVert\int\frac{Im\{\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\zeta-\partial_{\beta}^{s}\zeta\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{2}}.

We split the integral into two parts: for
|\alpha-\beta|\geq\sqrt{t}
, there holds

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{|\alpha-\beta|\geq\sqrt{t}}\frac{Im\{\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\zeta-\partial_{\beta}^{s}\zeta\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\int_{|\alpha-\beta|\geq\sqrt{t}}\frac{|D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)|}{(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}d\beta

\displaystyle\ \ \ +C\left\lVert\int_{|\alpha-\beta|\geq\sqrt{t}}\frac{|\partial_{\beta}^{s}\zeta D_{t}\bar{\zeta}|}{(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}+C\left\lVert\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(|\alpha|\geq\sqrt{t})}\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\zeta D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

We have used the Young’s inequality here. For
|\alpha-\beta|<\sqrt{t}
, note that
|\alpha|\leq t
implies
|\beta|\leq 2t
and we derive

\left\lVert\int_{|\alpha-\beta|<\sqrt{t}}\frac{Im\{\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\zeta-\partial_{\beta}^{s}\zeta\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|\alpha|\leq t)}\leq C\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s+1}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|\alpha|\leq 2t)}\left\lVert D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|\leq 2t)};

by the relevant part in the discussion for
\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}
we know that
\left\lVert D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|\leq 2t)}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. This gives

\left\lVert\int_{|\alpha-\beta|<\sqrt{t}}\frac{Im\{\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\zeta-\partial_{\beta}^{s}\zeta\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|\alpha|\leq t)}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

For
|\alpha|>t
, there holds
|\beta|>t/2
and we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{|\alpha-\beta|<\sqrt{t}}\frac{Im\{\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\zeta-\partial_{\beta}^{s}\zeta\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|\alpha|>t)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\int_{|\alpha-\beta|<\sqrt{t}}\frac{Im\{\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}[2(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)-\theta_{\alpha}]-\partial_{\beta}^{s-1}[2(\zeta_{\beta}-1)-\theta_{\beta}]\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|\alpha|>t)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\int_{|\alpha-\beta|<\sqrt{t}}\frac{Im\{\partial_{\alpha}^{s-1}\theta_{\alpha}-\partial_{\beta}^{s-1}\theta_{\beta}\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{2}(|\alpha|>t)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\left\lVert 2(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)-\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\left\lVert D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}+C\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|>t/2)}\left\lVert D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Now we conclude that

\left\lVert\int\frac{Im\{\partial_{\alpha}^{s}\zeta-\partial_{\beta}^{s}\zeta\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}

which implies
\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta-Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. The proof for
W^{s-2,\infty}
is quite similar and we shall write out the only difference:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{|\alpha-\beta|<\sqrt{t}}\frac{Im\{\partial_{\alpha}^{s-3}[2(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)-\theta_{\alpha}]-\partial_{\beta}^{s-3}[2(\zeta_{\beta}-1)-\theta_{\beta}]\}}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}\zeta_{\beta}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}(\beta)d\beta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|>t)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\left\lVert 2(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)-\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{W^{s-1,\infty}}\left\lVert D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}+C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{2}{3}}\ln t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{4}}\left\lVert 2(\zeta_{\alpha}-1)-\theta_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{H^{s}}+C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{2}{3}}\ln t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{2}{3}}\ln t.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

The advantage of
Q_{\alpha}
and
\theta_{\alpha}
lies in the fact that we have control over
\Lambda\Omega_{0}Q_{\alpha}
and
\Omega_{0}\theta_{\alpha}
, whereas direct estimates for
\Lambda\Omega_{0}D_{t}\zeta
and
\Omega_{0}D_{t}^{2}\zeta
are not available.
As indicated in Proposition
4.1
, whenever necessary, we may substitute
D_{t}\zeta
and
D_{t}^{2}\zeta
with
Q_{\alpha}
and
\theta_{\alpha}
respectively, and the error term behaves better than the leading term. For simplicity, we will continue to use the notation
D_{t}\zeta
and
D_{t}^{2}\zeta
, even when these substitutions are implicitly applied.

Also, the
W^{s-2,\infty}
estimate in Proposition
4.1
can be improved given
|\alpha|
is near
t
.

Proposition
4.1
allows us to apply Lemma
4.3
to
D_{t}\zeta
to derive the decay for
\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|\leq Ct^{1+\mu})}
.

Corollary 4.1
.

Suppose that
\mu>-\frac{1}{2}+\delta_{0}
. We have

(4.23)

\left\lVert D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|\leq Ct^{1+\mu})}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu}.

And for
1\leq k\leq s
,

(4.24)

\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{k}(\zeta-\alpha)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|\geq Ct^{1-\mu})}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu}.

(4.25)

\left\lVert\partial_{\alpha}^{k}D_{t}\zeta\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(|\alpha|\geq Ct^{1-\mu})}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu}.

Proof.

Write

D_{t}\zeta=Q_{\alpha}+O(\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{2}})=P_{\geq t^{-2}}Q_{\alpha}+P_{<t^{-2}}Q_{\alpha}

and note that

P_{\geq t^{-2}}Q_{\alpha}=-\frac{2\alpha}{it}\partial_{t}P_{\geq t^{-2}}Q_{\alpha}+\frac{2}{it}\Omega_{0}(P_{\geq t^{-2}}Q_{\alpha})

hence, according to (
4.9
),

\left\lVert P_{\geq t^{-2}}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu}+C\epsilon t^{-1+\delta_{0}}\ln t\leq C\epsilon t^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mu}.

Also, there holds

\left\lVert P_{<t^{-2}}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leq Ct^{-1}\left\lVert P_{<t^{-2}}Q_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\epsilon t^{-1}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Define
\tilde{\sigma}=D_{t}\theta
and we will need the following expansion of
D_{t}\theta
and
D_{t}\tilde{\sigma}
in Chapter 5:

Lemma 4.8
.

There holds

(4.26)

D_{t}\theta=D_{t}(I-\mathcal{H})(\zeta-\bar{\zeta})=2D_{t}\zeta-2b-(\bar{\mathcal{H}}+\mathcal{H})D_{t}(\zeta-\alpha)-[D_{t},\bar{\mathcal{H}}+\mathcal{H}](\zeta-\alpha).

and thus, if we assume Lemma
4.13
for a while,

D_{t}\bar{\theta}=2D_{t}\bar{\zeta}+O(\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}})_{H^{s}}.

Proof.

First, we know that
\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}
from Lemma
4.13
; next, straight calculation yields

(\bar{\mathcal{H}}+\mathcal{H})f=\left(\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}+\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}\right)f+\bar{\mathcal{H}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}\right)f+\mathcal{H}\left(1-\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}\right)f.

And it’s enough to consider
f=D_{t}\bar{\zeta}
since
D_{t}\alpha=b
behaves better. First we have

\left\|\left(\bar{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}+\mathcal{H}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}\right)D_{t}\zeta\right\|_{H^{s}}=C\left\|\int\frac{\eta(\alpha)-\eta(\beta)}{|\zeta(\alpha)-\zeta(\beta)|^{2}}D_{t}\bar{\zeta}d\beta\right\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Also, by the
L^{2}
boundedness of
\mathcal{H}
and the bootstrap assumptions,

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{\mathcal{H}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}\right)D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\left\lVert(1-\frac{1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}})D_{t}\bar{\zeta}\right\rVert_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Similar process applies to
\mathcal{H}\left(1-\frac{1}{\zeta_{\alpha}}\right)D_{t}\bar{\zeta}
, so we conclude that
\|(\bar{\mathcal{H}}+\mathcal{H})D_{t}(\bar{\zeta}-\alpha)\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\epsilon^{2}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

For the last term in (
4.26
), we have

[D_{t},\mathcal{H}](\bar{\zeta}-\alpha)=[D_{t}\zeta,\mathcal{H}]\frac{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1}{\zeta_{\alpha}},\ [D_{t},\bar{\mathcal{H}}](\bar{\zeta}-\alpha)=[D_{t}\bar{\zeta},\bar{\mathcal{H}}]\frac{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}-1}{\bar{\zeta}_{\alpha}}

and thus
